Rizal">Angono, Rizal</a>, <a href="/wiki/Philippines" title="Philippines">Philippines</a></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below"><a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_(disambiguation)" <p><b>Pope Clement I</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Clemens Romanus</i>; <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Ancient Greek language">Ancient Greek</a>: <span lang="grc">Κλήμης Ῥώμης</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Ancient_Greek" class="mw-redirect" title="Romanization of Ancient Greek">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Ancient Greek-language romanization"><i lang="grc-Latn">Klēmēs Rōmēs</i></span>) (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;35 AD</span> – 99 AD) was <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Rome" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Rome">bishop of Rome</a> in the late first century AD. He is listed by <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tertullian" title="Tertullian">Tertullian</a> as the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Rome" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Rome">bishop of Rome</a>, holding office from 88 AD to his death in 99 AD.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He is considered to be the first <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Father" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostolic Father">Apostolic Father</a> of the Church, one of the three chief ones together with <a href="/wiki/Polycarp" title="Polycarp">Polycarp</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ignatius_of_Antioch" title="Ignatius of Antioch">Ignatius of Antioch</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CE_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CE-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Few details are known about Clement's life. Clement was said to have been consecrated by <a href="/wiki/Peter_the_Apostle" class="mw-redirect" title="Peter the Apostle">Peter the Apostle</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-CE_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CE-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> and he is known to have been a leading member of the church in Rome in the late <a href="/wiki/Christianity_in_the_1st_century" title="Christianity in the 1st century">1st century</a>. Early church lists place him as the second or third<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> bishop of Rome after Peter. The <i><a href="/wiki/Liber_Pontificalis" title="Liber Pontificalis">Liber Pontificalis</a></i> states that Clement died in <a href="/wiki/Greece" title="Greece">Greece</a> in the third year of <a href="/wiki/Roman_emperor" title="Roman emperor">Emperor</a> <a href="/wiki/Trajan" title="Trajan">Trajan</a>'s reign, or 101 AD. <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a>, in his book <a href="/wiki/Church_History_(Eusebius)" title="Church History (Eusebius)">Church History</a>, mentions Clement as the third bishop of Rome and the "co-laborer" of <a href="/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle" title="Paul the Apostle">Paul</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement's only genuine extant writing is his letter to the church at <a href="/wiki/Roman_Corinth" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Corinth">Corinth</a> (<a href="/wiki/1_Clement" class="mw-redirect" title="1 Clement">1 Clement</a>) in response to a dispute in which certain <a href="/wiki/Presbyter" title="Presbyter">presbyters</a> of the Corinthian church had been deposed.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He asserted the authority of the presbyters as rulers of the church on the ground that the <a href="/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament" title="Apostles in the New Testament">Apostles</a> had appointed such.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> His letter, which is one of the oldest extant Christian documents outside the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>, was read in church, along with other epistles, some of which later <a href="/wiki/Development_of_the_Christian_Biblical_canon" class="mw-redirect" title="Development of the Christian Biblical canon">became part of the Christian canon</a>. These works were the first to affirm the apostolic authority of the clergy.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> A second epistle, <a href="/wiki/2_Clement" class="mw-redirect" title="2 Clement">2 Clement</a>, was once controversially attributed to Clement, although recent scholarship suggests it to be a <a href="/wiki/Homily" title="Homily">homily</a> by another author.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> In the legendary <a href="/wiki/Clementine_literature" title="Clementine literature">Clementine literature</a>, Clement is the intermediary through whom the apostles teach the church.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to tradition, Clement was imprisoned under the <a href="/wiki/Emperor_Trajan" class="mw-redirect" title="Emperor Trajan">Emperor Trajan</a>; during this time he is recorded to have led a ministry among fellow prisoners. Thereafter he was executed by being tied to an anchor and thrown into the sea.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Clement is recognized as a saint in many Christian churches and is considered a patron saint of mariners. He is commemorated on 23 November in the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_Saints_(Lutheran)" class="mw-redirect" title="Calendar of Saints (Lutheran)">Lutheran Church</a>. In <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Christianity" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Orthodox Christianity">Eastern Orthodox Christianity</a> his feast is kept on 24 or 25 November.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:San_clemente_fresco.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/San_clemente_fresco.jpg/220px-San_clemente_fresco.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/San_clemente_fresco.jpg/330px-San_clemente_fresco.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/San_clemente_fresco.jpg/440px-San_clemente_fresco.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2298" data-file-height="1615" /></a><figcaption>11th-century fresco in the Basilica of San Clemente, Rome: Saints Cyril and Methodius bring Saint Clement's relics to Rome</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <i><a href="/wiki/Liber_Pontificalis" title="Liber Pontificalis">Liber Pontificalis</a></i><sup id="cite_ref-Loomis19166_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Loomis19166-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> presents a list that makes <a href="/wiki/Pope_Linus" title="Pope Linus">Linus</a> the second in the line of bishops of Rome, with Peter as first; but at the same time it states that Peter ordained two bishops, Linus and <a href="/wiki/Anacletus" class="mw-redirect" title="Anacletus">Anacletus</a>, for the priestly service of the community, devoting himself instead to prayer and preaching, and that it was to Clement that he entrusted the Church as a whole, appointing him as his successor. Tertullian considered Clement to be the immediate successor of Peter.<sup id="cite_ref-Tertullian1903p258_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tertullian1903p258-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In one of his works, <a href="/wiki/Jerome" title="Jerome">Jerome</a> listed Clement as "the fourth bishop of Rome after Peter, if indeed the second was Linus and the third Anacletus, although most of the Latins think that Clement was second after the apostle."<sup id="cite_ref-Jerome_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jerome-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Clement is put after Linus and Cletus/Anacletus in the earliest (c.&#160;180) account, that of <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Irenaeus_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Irenaeus-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> who is followed by <a href="/wiki/Eusebius_of_Caesarea" class="mw-redirect" title="Eusebius of Caesarea">Eusebius of Caesarea</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Eusebius_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eusebius-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Early succession lists name Clement as the first,<sup id="cite_ref-Schaff_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Schaff-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 636">&#58;&#8202;636&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> second, or third<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> successor of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Peter</a>. However, the meaning of his inclusion in these lists has been very controversial.<sup id="cite_ref-Bishop_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bishop-14">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Some believe there were presbyter-bishops as early as the 1st century,<sup id="cite_ref-Bishop_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bishop-14">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> but that there is no evidence for a monarchical episcopacy in Rome at such an early date.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> There is also, however, no evidence of a change occurring in ecclesiastical organization in the latter half of the 2nd century, which would indicate that a new or newly-monarchical episcopacy was establishing itself.<sup id="cite_ref-Bishop_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bishop-14">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A tradition that began in the 3rd and 4th century,<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> has identified him as the Clement that Paul mentioned in <a href="/wiki/Philippians" class="mw-redirect" title="Philippians">Philippians</a> <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Philippians#4:3" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Philippians">4:3</a>, a fellow laborer in Christ.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup>  While in the mid-19th century it was customary to identify him as a freedman of <a href="/wiki/Titus_Flavius_Clemens_(consul)" title="Titus Flavius Clemens (consul)">Titus Flavius Clemens</a>, who was <a href="/wiki/Consul" title="Consul">consul</a> with his cousin, the Emperor <a href="/wiki/Domitian" title="Domitian">Domitian</a>, this identification, which no ancient sources suggest, afterwards lost support.<sup id="cite_ref-CE_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CE-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> The 2nd-century <i><a href="/wiki/Shepherd_of_Hermas" class="mw-redirect" title="Shepherd of Hermas">Shepherd of Hermas</a></i> mentions a Clement whose office it was to communicate with other churches; most likely, this is a reference to Clement I.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A large congregation existed in Rome c.&#160;58, when Paul wrote his Epistle to the Romans.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Paul arrived in Rome c.&#160;60 (Acts).<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Paul and Peter were said to have been martyred there. Nero persecuted Roman Christians after Rome burned in 64, and the congregation may have suffered further persecution under Domitian (81–96). Clement was the first of early Rome's most notable bishops.<sup id="cite_ref-CrossLivingstone2005p1422_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrossLivingstone2005p1422-17">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> The <i><a href="/wiki/Liber_Pontificalis" title="Liber Pontificalis">Liber Pontificalis</a></i>, which documents the reigns of popes, states that Clement had known Peter.
</p><p>Clement is known for his epistle to the church in Corinth (c.&#160;96), in which he asserts the apostolic authority of the bishops/presbyters as rulers of the church.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> The epistle mentions <i>episkopoi</i> (overseers, bishops) or <i>presbyteroi</i> (elders, presbyters) as the upper class of minister, served by the deacons, but, since it does not mention himself, it gives no indication of the title or titles used for Clement in Rome.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Saintclementmartyr.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Saintclementmartyr.png/170px-Saintclementmartyr.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="111" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Saintclementmartyr.png/255px-Saintclementmartyr.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Saintclementmartyr.png/340px-Saintclementmartyr.png 2x" data-file-width="731" data-file-height="478" /></a><figcaption>Martyrdom of St Clement by <a href="/wiki/Fungai" class="mw-redirect" title="Fungai">Fungai</a></figcaption></figure>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_legends_of_final_days">Death and legends of final days</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Death and legends of final days">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>According to apocryphal <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_the_Martyrs" title="Acts of the Martyrs"><i>acta</i></a> dating to the 4th century at earliest, Clement was banished from Rome to the <a href="/wiki/Chersonesos_Taurica" class="mw-redirect" title="Chersonesos Taurica">Chersonesus</a> during the reign of the Emperor <a href="/wiki/Trajan" title="Trajan">Trajan</a><sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CE_3-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CE-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> and was set to work in a stone quarry. Finding on his arrival that the prisoners were suffering from lack of water, he knelt down in prayer. Looking up, he saw a lamb on a hill, went to where the lamb had stood and struck the ground with his pickaxe, releasing a gushing stream of clear water. This miracle resulted in the conversion of large numbers of the local pagans and his fellow prisoners to Christianity. As punishment, Clement was <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyred</a> by being tied to an anchor<sup id="cite_ref-Stracke_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stracke-18">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> and thrown from a boat into the <a href="/wiki/Black_Sea" title="Black Sea">Black Sea</a>. The legend recounts that every year a miraculous ebbing of the sea revealed a divinely built shrine containing his bones. However, the oldest sources on Clement's life, <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jerome" title="Jerome">Jerome</a>, note nothing of his martyrdom.<sup id="cite_ref-Schaff_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Schaff-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 639">&#58;&#8202;639&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Inkerman_Cave_Monastery" title="Inkerman Cave Monastery">Inkerman Cave Monastery</a> marks the supposed place of Clement's burial in <a href="/wiki/Crimea" title="Crimea">Crimea</a>. A year or two before his own death in 869, <a href="/wiki/Cyril_and_Methodius" title="Cyril and Methodius">Cyril</a> brought to Rome what he believed to be the <a href="/wiki/Relics" class="mw-redirect" title="Relics">relics</a> of Clement, bones he found in Crimea buried with an anchor on dry land. They are now enshrined in the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_di_San_Clemente" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica di San Clemente">Basilica di San Clemente</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CE_3-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CE-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> But there are also other traditions<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> about an ancient veneration of the relics in <a href="/wiki/Chersonesus" title="Chersonesus">Chersonesus</a> and the translation of the head to <a href="/wiki/Kyiv" title="Kyiv">Kyiv</a>. Other relics of Clement, including his head, are claimed by the <a href="/wiki/Kyiv_Monastery_of_the_Caves" class="mw-redirect" title="Kyiv Monastery of the Caves">Kyiv Monastery of the Caves</a> in Ukraine.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The <i>Liber Pontificalis</i> states that Clement wrote two letters (though the second letter, 2 Clement, is no longer ascribed to him by many modern scholars).<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CE_3-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CE-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-McBrien_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McBrien-20">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Epistle_of_Clement">Epistle of Clement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Epistle of Clement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/First_Epistle_of_Clement" title="First Epistle of Clement">First Epistle of Clement</a></div>
<p>Clement's only extant, uncontested text is a letter to the <a href="/wiki/Early_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Early Church">Christian congregation</a> in <a href="/wiki/Roman_Corinth" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Corinth">Corinth</a>, often called the <a href="/wiki/First_Epistle_of_Clement" title="First Epistle of Clement">First Epistle of Clement</a> or 1 Clement. The history of 1 Clement clearly and continuously shows Clement as the author of this letter. It is considered the earliest authentic Christian document outside the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>.
</p><p>Clement writes to the troubled congregation in Corinth, where certain "presbyters" or "bishops" have been deposed (the class of clergy above that of deacons is argued by certain historians to be designated indifferently by the two terms).<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Clement calls for repentance and reinstatement of those who have been deposed, in line with maintenance of order and obedience to church authority, since the <a href="/wiki/Twelve_Apostles" class="mw-redirect" title="Twelve Apostles">apostles</a> established the ministry of "bishops and deacons."<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He mentions "offering the gifts" as one of the functions of the higher class of clergy.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> The epistle offers valuable insight into Church ministry at that time and into the history of the Roman Church.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> It was highly regarded, and was read in church at Corinth along with the Scriptures c.&#160;170.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>We should be obedient unto God, rather than follow those who in arrogance and unruliness have set themselves up as leaders in abominable jealousy.... For Christ is with them that are lowly of mind, not with them that exalt themselves over the flock.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><a href="#CITEREFClement_of_Rome1885b">Clement of Rome 1885b</a>, 1 Clem 14:1; 16:1</cite></div></blockquote>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Do we then think it to be a great and marvelous thing, if the Creator of the universe shall bring about the resurrection of them that have served Him with holiness in the assurance of a good faith, seeing that He showeth to us even by a bird the magnificence of His promise?</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><a href="#CITEREFClement_of_Rome1885b">Clement of Rome 1885b</a>, 1 Clem 26:1</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>In the epistle, it is argued by some that Clement uses the terms "bishop" and "presbyter" interchangeably for the higher order of ministers above deacons.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> In some congregations, particularly in Egypt, the distinction between bishops and presbyters seems to have become established only later.<sup id="cite_ref-CrossLivingstone2005p210_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrossLivingstone2005p210-21">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> But by the middle of the second century all the leading Christian centres had bishops.<sup id="cite_ref-CrossLivingstone2005p210_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrossLivingstone2005p210-21">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>  Scholars such as Bart Ehrman treat as significant the fact that, of the seven letters written by <a href="/wiki/Ignatius_of_Antioch" title="Ignatius of Antioch">Ignatius of Antioch</a> to seven Christian churches shortly after the time of Clement, the only one that does not present the church as headed by a single bishop is that addressed to the church in Rome, although this letter did not refer to a collective priesthood either.<sup id="cite_ref-Ehrman2008p83_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ehrman2008p83-22">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement's letter also contains historical references, it mentions persecutions of Christians, records the martyrdom of the <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Apostle Peter</a> and suggests that the apostle Paul traveled to <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-newadvent.org_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-newadvent.org-23">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Theology">Theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pope_Clement_I.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Pope_Clement_I.jpg/220px-Pope_Clement_I.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="310" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Pope_Clement_I.jpg/330px-Pope_Clement_I.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/3e/Pope_Clement_I.jpg 2x" data-file-width="432" data-file-height="608" /></a><figcaption>Clement of Rome</figcaption></figure>
<p>Clement's view on justification has had much scholarly discussion, as he is sometimes argued to have believed <a href="/wiki/Sola_fide" title="Sola fide">sola fide</a>, though others believe him as having synergist views. Debate exists, because Clement directly stated that "we are not justified by ourselves but by faith", however in other places of the letter, he stresses judgement on sin.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-24">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Protestant scholar Tom Schreiner argued that Clement of Rome believed in a grace oriented justification by faith, which will cause the believer to do works as a result,<sup id="cite_ref-:0_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-25">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Philip_Schaff" title="Philip Schaff">Philip Schaff</a> also said that Clement probably taught a faith alone doctrine<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> while <i><a href="/wiki/Catholic_Encyclopedia" title="Catholic Encyclopedia">Catholic Encyclopedia</a></i> wrote that Clement believed works to be part of justification.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Rudolf Knopf and Rudolf Bultmann also believed that Clement believed in <a href="/wiki/Synergism" title="Synergism">synergism</a>, and that the believer needs to cooperate with the grace of God to be saved. Rudolf Knopf in his commentary on the letter of Clement to the Corinthians stated that: <i>"Pre-Christian sins are wiped out by baptism. For those sins that follow, a person must have faith in divine mercy and, at the same time, that person must exhibit his or her own good deeds, apart from which the person cannot be saved"</i><sup id="cite_ref-:1_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-24">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> David Downs argued against the view that Clement of Rome holds synergist views, he argued that Clement did not write a letter about deep soteriology, but instead to provide moral guidance to the Corinthians, David Downs stated "According to the soteriological economy of Clement everything rests on the goodness, mercy, and election of the Creator, which have befitted the 'chosen portion' through Jesus".<sup id="cite_ref-:1_24-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-24">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<p><a href="/wiki/Thomas_R._Schreiner" title="Thomas R. Schreiner">Thomas Schreiner</a> argued that Clement taught that faith was enough to be saved because of 1 Clement 32:4 where he stated:<sup id="cite_ref-:2_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-28">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup></p><blockquote><p>And so we, having been called through His will in Christ Jesus, are not justified through ourselves or through our own wisdom or understanding or piety or works which we wrought in holiness of heart, but through faith, whereby the Almighty God justified all men that have been from the beginning; to whom be the glory for ever and ever. Amen .- 1 Clement 32:4<sup id="cite_ref-:2_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-28">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p>The epistle has been cited as the first work to establish <a href="/wiki/Primacy_of_the_Roman_Pontiff" class="mw-redirect" title="Primacy of the Roman Pontiff">Roman primacy</a>, because he wrote to settle a problem in the church,<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> but most scholars see the epistle as more fraternal than authoritative,<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Orthodox</a> scholar <a href="/wiki/John_Meyendorff" title="John Meyendorff">John Meyendorff</a> sees it as connected with the Roman church's awareness of its "priority" (rather than "primacy") among local churches.<sup id="cite_ref-Meyendorff1992_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Meyendorff1992-31">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> It has also been argued by Dave Armstrong, that Clement supported <a href="/wiki/Papal_Infallibility" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal Infallibility">Papal Infallibility</a> in Letter to the Corinthians 1, 63. Because of him speaking of the Corinthians to "being obedient" to the things he has "written through the Holy Spirit" in order to correct and "root out the wicked passion of jealousy".<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <i>Catholic Encyclopedia</i>, the letter of Clement has <a href="/wiki/Trinity" title="Trinity">Trinitarian theology</a> and Christ is frequently called as the <a href="/wiki/High_Priest_of_Israel" title="High Priest of Israel">high priest</a> by him.<sup id="cite_ref-newadvent.org_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-newadvent.org-23">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings_formerly_attributed_to_Clement">Writings formerly attributed to Clement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Writings formerly attributed to Clement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Giovanni_Battista_Tiepolo_094.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Giovanni_Battista_Tiepolo_094.jpg/220px-Giovanni_Battista_Tiepolo_094.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="285" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Giovanni_Battista_Tiepolo_094.jpg/330px-Giovanni_Battista_Tiepolo_094.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Giovanni_Battista_Tiepolo_094.jpg/440px-Giovanni_Battista_Tiepolo_094.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2024" data-file-height="2623" /></a><figcaption><i>Saint Clement</i>, by <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Battista_Tiepolo" title="Giovanni Battista Tiepolo">Tiepolo</a></figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_Epistle_of_Clement">Second Epistle of Clement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Second Epistle of Clement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Second_Epistle_of_Clement" title="Second Epistle of Clement">Second Epistle of Clement</a></div>
<p>The Second Epistle of Clement is a homily, or sermon, likely written in Corinth or Rome, but not by Clement.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Early Christian congregations often shared homilies to be read. The homily describes Christian character and repentance.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> It is possible that the Church from which Clement sent his epistle had included a festal homily to share in one economical post, thus the homily became known as the Second Epistle of Clement.
</p><p>While 2 Clement has been traditionally ascribed to Clement, most scholars believe that 2 Clement was written in the 2nd century based on the doctrinal themes of the text and a near match between words in 2 Clement and in the <a href="/wiki/Greek_Gospel_of_the_Egyptians" title="Greek Gospel of the Egyptians">Greek Gospel of the Egyptians</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-CE_3-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CE-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-McBrien_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McBrien-20">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Epistles_on_Virginity">Epistles on Virginity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Epistles on Virginity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Two "Epistles on Virginity" were traditionally attributed to Clement, but now there exists almost universal consensus that Clement was not the author of those two epistles.<sup id="cite_ref-Virginity_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Virginity-34">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="False_Decretals">False Decretals</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: False Decretals">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Pseudo-Isidore" title="Pseudo-Isidore">Pseudo-Isidore</a></div>
<p>A 9th-century collection of church legislation known as the False Decretals, which was once attributed to <a href="/wiki/Isidore_of_Seville" title="Isidore of Seville">Isidore of Seville</a>, is largely composed of forgeries. All of what it presents as letters of pre-<a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Nicaea" title="First Council of Nicaea">Nicene</a> popes, beginning with Clement, are forgeries, as are some of the documents that it attributes to councils;<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;f&#93;</a></sup> and more than forty falsifications are found in the decretals that it gives as those of post-Nicene popes from <a href="/wiki/Sylvester_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Sylvester I">Sylvester I</a> (314–335) to <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_II" title="Pope Gregory II">Gregory II</a> (715–731). The False Decretals were part of a series of falsifications of past legislation by a party in the Carolingian Empire whose principal aim was to free the church and the bishops from interference by the state and the <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_archbishop#Roman_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Metropolitan archbishop">metropolitan archbishops</a> respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunson2004345_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunson2004345-37">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CrossLivingstone2005p601_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrossLivingstone2005p601-38">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement is included among other early Christian popes as authors of the Pseudo-Isidoran (or False) Decretals, a 9th-century forgery. These decrees and letters portray even the early popes as claiming absolute and universal authority.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;g&#93;</a></sup> Clement is the earliest pope to whom a Pseudo-Isidoran text is attributed.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Clementine_literature">Clementine literature</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Clementine literature">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Clementine_literature" title="Clementine literature">Clementine literature</a></div>
<p>Clement is also the hero of an early Christian romance or <a href="/wiki/Novel" title="Novel">novel</a> that has survived in at least two different versions, known as the Clementine literature, where he is identified with Emperor <a href="/wiki/Domitian" title="Domitian">Domitian</a>'s cousin <a href="/wiki/Titus_Flavius_Clemens_(consul)" title="Titus Flavius Clemens (consul)">Titus Flavius Clemens</a>. Clementine literature portrays Clement as the Apostles' means of disseminating their teachings to the Church.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_self_2-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC_self-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Recognition_as_a_saint">Recognition as a saint</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Recognition as a saint">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:StClement_Church_Moscow_01-2016_img2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/StClement_Church_Moscow_01-2016_img2.jpg/220px-StClement_Church_Moscow_01-2016_img2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="275" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/StClement_Church_Moscow_01-2016_img2.jpg/330px-StClement_Church_Moscow_01-2016_img2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/StClement_Church_Moscow_01-2016_img2.jpg/440px-StClement_Church_Moscow_01-2016_img2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2873" data-file-height="3591" /></a><figcaption>St. Clement is one of the few Roman popes to have <a href="/wiki/St._Clement%27s_Church_in_Moscow" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Clement&#39;s Church in Moscow">a Russian Orthodox church</a> dedicated in his name.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Clement's name is in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Canon" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Canon">Roman Canon</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Mass_(liturgy)" title="Mass (liturgy)">Mass</a>. He is commemorated on 23 November as a <a href="/wiki/Pope" title="Pope">pope</a> and <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> as well as within the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Lutheran_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran Church">Lutheran Church</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Syriac_Orthodox_Church" title="Syriac Orthodox Church">Syriac Orthodox Church</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Malankara_Orthodox_Syrian_Church" title="Malankara Orthodox Syrian Church">Malankara Orthodox Syrian Church</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Macedonian_Orthodox_Church" title="Macedonian Orthodox Church">Macedonian Orthodox Church</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church" title="Greek Orthodox Church">Greek Orthodox Church</a>, as well as the <a href="/wiki/Syriac_Catholic_Church" title="Syriac Catholic Church">Syriac Catholic Church</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Syro-Malankara_Catholic_Church" title="Syro-Malankara Catholic Church">Syro-Malankara Catholic Church</a> and all <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite" title="Byzantine Rite">Byzantine Rite</a> <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_Churches" title="Eastern Catholic Churches">Eastern Catholic Churches</a> commemorate Clement of Rome (called in <a href="/wiki/Syriac_language" title="Syriac language">Syriac</a> <i>"Mor Clemis"</i>) on 24 November; the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church" title="Russian Orthodox Church">Russian Orthodox Church</a> commemorates Clement on 25 November. Clement is honored in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Church of England</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> on <a href="/wiki/November_23" title="November 23">23 November</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/St_Clement%27s_Church,_Moscow" title="St Clement&#39;s Church, Moscow">St Clement's Church</a> in Moscow is renowned for its glittering Baroque interior and iconostasis, as well as a set of gilded 18th-century railings. The parish was disbanded in 1934 and the original free-standing gate was demolished. The Lenin State Library stored its books in the building throughout the Soviet period. It was not until 2008 that the building reverted to the Russian Orthodox Church.
</p><p>Clement of Rome is commemorated in the Synaxarium of the <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria">Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria</a> on the 29th of the month of Hatour [25 November (Julian) – equivalent to 8 December (Gregorian) due to the current 13-day Julian–Gregorian Calendar offset]. According to the Coptic Church Synaxarium, he suffered martyrdom in AD 100 during the reign of Emperor Trajan (98–117). He was martyred by tying his neck to an anchor and casting him into the sea. The record of the 29th of the Coptic month of Hatour states that this saint was born in Rome to an honorable father whose name was Fostinus and also states that he was a member of the Roman senate and that his father educated him and taught him Greek literature.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relics">Relics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Relics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Besides relics venerated in Rome and Kyiv (see <a class="mw-selflink-fragment" href="#Death_and_legends_of_final_days">above</a>), 
in the city of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Cruz_de_Tenerife" title="Santa Cruz de Tenerife">Santa Cruz de Tenerife</a> in <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Tibia" title="Tibia">shinbone</a> of Clement is kept. It was a gift of Sidotti, <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Antioch" title="Patriarch of Antioch">Patriarch of Antioch</a>, to the <a href="/wiki/Iglesia_de_la_Concepci%C3%B3n_(Santa_Cruz_de_Tenerife)" title="Iglesia de la Concepción (Santa Cruz de Tenerife)">Church of the Immaculate Conception</a>. Historically, this was a highly revered relic in the city.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGonzález20073_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonzález20073-42">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Symbolism">Symbolism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Symbolism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mariner%27s_Cross.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Mariner%27s_Cross.svg/220px-Mariner%27s_Cross.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="412" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Mariner%27s_Cross.svg/330px-Mariner%27s_Cross.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Mariner%27s_Cross.svg/440px-Mariner%27s_Cross.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="383" data-file-height="717" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Anchored_Cross" class="mw-redirect" title="Anchored Cross">Anchored Cross</a>, also known as Mariner's or Saint Clement's cross.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In workings of art, Clement can be recognized by having an anchor at his side or tied to his neck. He is most often depicted wearing <a href="/wiki/Papal_vestments" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal vestments">papal vestments</a>, including the pallium, and sometimes with a <a href="/wiki/Papal_tiara" title="Papal tiara">papal tiara</a> but more often with a <a href="/wiki/Mitre" title="Mitre">mitre</a>. He is also sometimes shown with papal symbols such as the <a href="/wiki/Papal_cross" title="Papal cross">papal cross</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Keys_of_Heaven" title="Keys of Heaven">Keys of Heaven</a>. In reference to his martyrdom, he often holds the palm of martyrdom.
</p><p>Clement can be seen depicted near a <a href="/wiki/Fountain" title="Fountain">fountain</a> or <a href="/wiki/Spring_(hydrosphere)" class="mw-redirect" title="Spring (hydrosphere)">spring</a>, relating to the incident from his <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiography</a>, or lying in a temple in the sea. The <a href="/wiki/Anchored_Cross" class="mw-redirect" title="Anchored Cross">Anchored Cross</a> or Mariner's Cross is also referred to as St. Clement's Cross, in reference to the way he was martyred.<sup id="cite_ref-Stracke_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stracke-18">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Rome&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-4"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-4">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFCampbell1907">Campbell 1907</a> details the debate regarding whether there was one pope with two names, or two distinct popes. Ancient sources are contradictory, and modern scholarship is divided.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-12"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-12">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Like Schaff, the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Holy See</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Annuario_Pontificio" title="Annuario Pontificio">Annuario Pontificio</a></i>, gives Clement as "supreme pontiff of Rome" in either 92–99 or 68–76, making him either the first or the third successor of Saint Peter, but not the second.(<a href="#CITEREFLibreria_Editrice_Vaticana2008">Libreria Editrice Vaticana 2008</a>, p.&#160;7)</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-13"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-13">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The <i>Catholic Encyclopedia</i> article says that only on the false assumption that "Cletus" and "Anacletus" were two distinct persons, instead of variations of the name of single individual, did some think that Clement was the fourth successor of Saint Peter.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-15"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-15">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFKellyWalsh2005">Kelly &amp; Walsh 2005</a>, p.&#160;7 note that "Writers of the 3rd and 4th centuries, like <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Jerome" title="Jerome">Jerome</a>, equate him (St. Clement I), perhaps, correctly, with the Clement whom St. Paul mentions (<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=Philippians%204:3&amp;version=nrsv">Phil 4:3</a>) as a fellow worker."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-30"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-30">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFPhan2000">Phan 2000</a>, p.&#160;32 writes, "Most scholars would now regard 1 Clement as an impressive example of fraternal correction rather than an authoritative intervention." </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-35"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-35">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The Encyclopædia Britannica places the <a href="/wiki/Donation_of_Constantine" title="Donation of Constantine">Donation of Constantine</a> in this section; the Oxford Dictionary of the Christian Church places it in the section of the pre-Nicene Popes.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-39"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-39">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFDurant2011">Durant 2011</a>, p.&#160;525 writes, "These early documents were designed to show that by the oldest traditions and practice of the Church no bishop might be deposed, no Church councils might be convened, and no major issue might be decided, without the consent of the pope. Even the early pontiffs, by these evidences, had claimed absolute and universal authority as vicars of Christ on Earth."</span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>

</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="navbox-group" style="background-color: gold;width:1%"><a href="/wiki/History_of_the_papacy" title="History of the papacy">History of the papacy</a></th><td class="navbox-list-with-group navbox-list navbox-odd" style="width:100%;padding:0"><div style="padding:0 0.25em"></div><table class="nowraplinks navbox-subgroup" style="border-spacing:0"><tbody><tr><th scope="row" class="navbox-group" style="background-color:gold;width:1%"><a href="/wiki/Papal_selection_before_1059" title="Papal selection before 1059">Antiquity and Early<br /> Middle Ages</a></th><td class="navbox-list-with-group navbox-list navbox-even hlist" style="width:100%;padding:0"><div style="padding:0 0.25em">
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Papal_selection_before_1059#During_the_Roman_Empire" title="Papal selection before 1059">During the Roman Empire</a> (until 493)
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Bishops_of_Rome_under_Constantine_the_Great" title="Bishops of Rome under Constantine the Great">Under Constantine</a> (312–337)</li></ul></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ostrogothic_Papacy" title="Ostrogothic Papacy">Ostrogothic Papacy</a> (493–537)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Papacy" title="Byzantine Papacy">Byzantine Papacy</a> (537–752)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Frankish_Papacy" title="Frankish Papacy">Frankish Papacy</a> (756–857)</li>
